[trialAccount t18360815-2000]
2000. JAMES WARD alias George Reaf was indicted for that he, having been convicted of unlawfully uttering counterfeit, coin, did, on the 21st of July, unlawfully, unjustly, deceitfully, and feloniously, utter and put off to John King the younger, a counterfeit shilling, well knowing it to be counterfeit.
MESSRS. BODKIN and CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution
WILLIAM WILLIAMS . In October, 1834, I was assistant to the town-clerk of Monmouth—I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of James Ward, which I have brought from the present town-clerk's officer—I was present when Ward was tried—the prisoner is the man ( read)
CHARLES BARBER . I am a green-grocer, and live in Jacob-street, Bermondsey. On the 6th of July, the prisoner came to my shop between nine and ten o'clock, and purchased half an ounce of tobacco—my wife served him—he threw a bad shilling down on the counter—I took it up, looked at it, and gave it to Evans the officer, who came in before the prisoner left—the prisoner then said he did not want any tobacco—I had given him the change, and the tobacco was in his hand—Evans took him away.
Prisoner Q. Was it you or your wife who took the shilling from me? A. It was me—you laid it on the counter, and I took it off—I did not put it into the till—I gave it to Evans—he gave it me back again, and I marked it—my wife gave you the change which was 6 1/4 d
JAMES CHRISTOPHER EVANS . I am in the police. On the 6th of July, I saw the prisoner standing opposite Barber's door—I watched him, and saw him go into the house—Mrs. Barber was serving and Mr. Barber was behind the counter—I went in about two minutes after the prisoner, and asked Barber what the man had given him—the tobacco laid on the counter—Barber threw down a shilling and said, "A bad shilling"—I examined it, and saw it was bad—I told Barber to mark it, and I then took possession of it—I have it now—I searched the prisoner and found one goods shilling and three good sixpences in his pocket, and 6 1/4 d. in copper, and about half an ounce of tobacco—I said, "I think you are passing bad money"—he said "No, I am quite a different sort of man; look at my hands, I work hard"—I said, "Where do you work?"—he said, "At the Railroad"—he said, this was all through drink—that he had been drinking—I could find no bad money on him and let him go—I kept the shilling.
JOHN KING . My father keeps the Three Compasses, in Bermondsey-new-road. On the 21st of July the prisoner came to my father's house and called for half a pint of porter and gave me a shilling—I put it to my mouth and bent it and immediately found it was bad—I showed it to my mother—she put it on the counter and told him it was bad, and he knew it—he
said he was a poor hard-working man, and had just taken at in his work, and pointed up the Kent-road—not in a direction to the Railroad—he did not drink the beer—I followed him out till I saw Wilkins, a policeman, and pointed him out to him—he followed him and I went home—Wilkins showed me the shilling next morning.
Prisoner Q. Did you not take the money out of your mother's presence for ten minutes, and come back and say it was bad? A. He was not in the house ten minutes—I took the shilling into the parlour and showed it to my mother—I told her it was bad, and she came out and told the prisoner so—I did not show it to any body else—I bit it before I showed it to my mother—I am sure it is the same shilling that I had back.
WILLIAM WILKINS . I am a policeman. I was in the neighbourhood of Bermondsey, and saw King following the prisoner on the 21st of July; he told me why he was following him—I told him to return home, and I followed the prisoner a short distance—I then stopped him and asked him what he had done with the bad shilling he had offered at the public house—he said he had thrown it away in the sewer by Bermondsay church—I searched him, and found a shilling concealed in the wristband of his shirt—it dropped from his wrist—it was a bent a shilling—I took him into custody—he said on the road that the had taken it taken it of Tim O'Leary, a gangsman at the Railroad—I have the shilling in my possession.
Prisoner Q. Did you take the shilling out of my clothes or out of the mad? A. It was quite dry where you dropped it—it was under a shelter—I saw it drop from your wrist.
TIMOTHY LEARY . I am an excavator. The prisoner worked for me, on the 21st of July, one day, on the Greenwich Railway—I never paid him any money—one of the men lent him 2 1/2 d., but no money was paid him.
CHARLES BARBER re-examined That is the shilling he offered me, and which I bit—I am quite certain.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. I have examined both the shillings—they are both counterfeit, and have been cast in the same mould.
GUILTY . Aged 24— Transported For Life.
Before Mr. Justice Williams
[trialAccount t18360815-2001]
2001. JAMES SULLIVAN was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Hugh Wood.
HUGH KEOUGH . I am a labourer. I knew the deceased Hugh Wood—he was a carman—on the day this happened, I was in Bridge-yard, helping him to load a cart with potatoes—there were six sacks—they were put in front, there on each side—he was in the front past of the cart, and I was behind—the body of the cart appeared as usual—there were two more sacks to come in, and the first of the two that was put into the cart tilted up, and we were both shot out—it was put on the back part of the cart at first, and directly the weight came on the tail-board, the cart turned up—the deceased was thrown right out of the cart, on his back, on the stones; and the potatoes were shot out of the sacks, and covered over him—I did not see what he fell on, for I was shot out myself—when he was picked up, I saw the blood on the back of his head—I was hurt—my leg was nearly broken—I did not lose my sense—he was taken to the hospital—I did not see him dead—after the cart tilted up, I went round and found the trap stick was out of its place, and was laid against the front of the cart—if it had been in its proper place—the cart not have. tilted up.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A. Two or three years—he was not employed by me—I have frequently seen the deceased and other carters in company—I do not know whether it was a common trick to pull out the trap-stick when the cart was empty—I was in the cart while it was being loaded—there were six sacks of potatoes in it before I went into it.
DANIEL JEWITT . I saw the cart—when the seventh sack was put in at the tail of the cart, up went the whole cart, and they were both shot out—after it was over, I saw the pin lying in the front of the cart out of its proper place—I saw the deceased at the hospital on the Monday following.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present while the cart was being loaded? A. I was present when they put the sack in, and it tripped up—I was not present at the commencement of the loading of the cart—I saw no one near the pin.
GEORGE BELL . I am in the employ of Mr. Smith. I saw the prisoner on the Monday after the accident—Wood was then at the hospital in a bad way—I heard the prisoner speadking to, other person in Mill-lane, and went up—he said he took the stick out, but it was not with the intention of doing any harm, as he had seen it done before—he was crying, and said he was very sorry.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you observed the cart? A. No—I saw it empty—it was waiting for a fare some time—any of the carters had an opportunity of taking out the pin while it was empty.
JAMES KEOUGH . I am a cowkeeper, and live in Dean-street, Tooley-street. The prisoner has worked for me for some time—I just came by after the accident happened—the prisoner was not there—I saw him on the Monday following—Wood was then dead—the prisoner said there were several persons, he thought, accused of pulling the trap-stick out, and he was the person who actually did do it, but not with intent to do him any harm as he had seen it done several times before; and I have seen it done myself by other people—that least weight will tilt a cart up when the pin is out—I should think the weight of a man getting into the cart would do it—the prisoner seemed very much affected.
Cross-examined. Q. He has been two or three years in your service? A. Yes, off and on—he is a well-conducted, quiet, industrious young man—I believe he was on very good terms with the deceased—this was a silly frolic.
WILLIAM ROBERT JONES . The deceased was brought to Guy's Hospital, one Saturday evening, between six and seven o'clock—I do not remember the day of the month—I believe it was about two months since—he was insensible—on any question being put to him, he would utter something quite unintelligible, but he became worse shortly after—he lived till between one and two o'clock on Sunday morning—after death, I examined the state of his head very minutely—I found a fracture in the back part of his head, extending from a point where I suppose a blow to have been received—the brain also had suffered from the violence of the blow, and there was blood effused from small vessels, ruptured—I should say his death was caused by injury on the head—his death was caused by the fracture of the skull which I have described—it was that, the effusion of blood, and concussion also—I have not a doubt of its being the cause of death.
Q. If a man was thrown out of a cart by its suddenly tilting, and
lighting on his head on the pavement, might the appearances you saw be produced? A. Yes—I think the whole of them.
GUILTY . Aged 18— Strongly Recommended to Mercy Fined 1 s ., and discharged.

2004. JOSEPH OAKLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of August, 1 bottle, value 2 d.; and 1 1/2 pint of wine, value 3 s.; the goods of Charles Bennett, his master.
CHARLES BENNETT . I live in Bedford-row, Walworth-road. The prisoner was in my employ, and carried out wine—the constable brought him back to me.
JOHN HAYNES ( police-constable P 129.) I took the prisoner, and found this bottle of wine on him—I did not say that it would be better for him to confess.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What did you say to him? A. I stopped him in the street and took the bottle from him—I said, "Where did you get this bottle of wine from?"—I took it from under his coat—he said, "For God's sake don't choke me, I will go quietly with you"—he was perfectly sober—I took him to his master.
CHARLES BENNETT . The prisoner was brought back—I believe the bottle to be mine—it was not sealed—as he was going to the station-house he admitted that he stole it—that he had robbed me, and begged I would not be hard with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he use that word? A. I think he did—I cannot take upon myself to swear he did—I had cut the letter "S" in the cork—he had been between five and six months in my service—he had a good character—I should say he was a little elevated.
GUILTY . Aged 19— Recommended to mercy — Confined Two Months.

[trialAccount t18360815-2006]
2006. MARY FRANCIS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of July, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of Joseph Stockes, from the person of Richard Searle Stokes.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution
NANCY STOKES . I am the wife of Joseph Stokes, and live in Belvidere-place, Southwark. On the 6th of July, about half-past three o'clock, I sent out Mary Jones with my little boy who is five months old—the child had a silk handkerchief on—I found it at the work-house, about half-past eight o'clock—my husband had gone to the work-house before me—the child then had no handkerchief on—this is the handkerchief that was on it child when sent out.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. What day in July was this? A. On Wednesday, on the 7th of July, to best of my recollection.
MARY ANN JONES . I received the child that Wednesday in July, near about half-past five o'clock—I went to Southwark-bridge-road—the prisoner came up to me—she hallooed after me, "Little girl"—I turned round—she said, "I wish you would go of an errand for me"—I said I could not, I was going home—she said, "You are going past the door, won't you take this ring to my husband?"—I again refused—the child was crying—I had it across my arms—the prisoner came to me, reached over my shoulder, and snatched the child from me—I was very much alarmed, and went into a tobacconist's shop, and told the people—the child had this handkerchief on.
Cross-examined. Q. What hour in the day was this? A. About half-past five, to the best of my knowledge, when she came up—I cannot speak to her clothes, but she had a green stuff—I never stated she had on a straw bonnet tied with brown ribbon, nor a green gown with boots buttoned inside, only a green gown—when I came home I told Mrs. Stokes so—I only looked at her face—she had a white bonnet on—it was straw—it was white—I did not say it was straw—I cannot tell what kind of boots—I never said she had boots—I cannot tell what shawl she had on—I never attempted to describe it to any one—I never said she had a red shawl—I did not tell my mistress what kind of bonnet she had on—I do not recollect that I told you she had a straw bonnet.
MR. DOANE. Q. To the best of your belief, is the prisoner the woman who took the child? A. Yes—I described her to Cowlin, the officer as fresh-coloured and red hair, and her hair braided and a green gown.
MARY FITZPATRICK . I am a widow, and reside at No. 1, Charles-street, Westminster-road. On Wednesday evening, the 6th of July, from five to six o'clock, I saw the prisoner coming down the Westminster-road with a young infant in her arms—she had two little girls, very young, by the side of her—she put the little boy across the little girl's lap, and said, "I am going for change; I will come back"—she turned very quick and walked off—I had some suspicions, and told the girls not to leave the child till I came back—I went her, and came up with her—I said, "Where are
you going, my good woman?"—she said, "What is that to you?—I said, "It is something to me; if you do not come back and take that child from those two children I will give your in charge"—she said, "It is not mine"—I said I never saw a woman once that I did not know again, and if she would not come back I would give her in charge—she came back and took the child and smothered it up—I said, "My good woman, there are plenty of workhouses where you can leave your child, if they are applied to".
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say you never saw a woman once that you did not know again? A. Yes—I took particular notice of her—she had a straw bonnet on, but I did not notice what ribbon—it was white straw and a green stuff gown—I cannot tell what kind of stuff—she had boots, I cannot say what colour—I took no notice—I observed nothing particular about her bonnet—I did not observe a feather, nor a red flower—it was between five and six o'clock on a Wednesday, in July—I had never seen the woman before—I told the policeman she was carrotty-haired woman, in a green gown, dark shawl, and straw bonnet—I did not tell him she had boots—it was a dark shawl, with a large flower at the corner—I did not take notice of the colour, or what kind of flower it was.
MARTHA COWDROY . I am a widow, and carry on the business of a pawnbroker in Frederick-place, Borough-road. On the Wednesday evening, between five and six o'clock, a female with a child in her arms came to pawn this handkerchief—I do not at all recollect the person—this is the handkerchief—she did not stay to take the duplicate—the child was crying very much—she said she would call again.
TIMOTHY MABER . I keep a public-house in New Cut, Lambeth. On this Wednesday evening I remember the prisoner coming to my shop with as infant in her arms—she had a pennyworth of gin, which she paid me for, and after waiting some time, she had half a pint of fourpenny ale—I am positive she is the person—it was from a quarter to twenty minutes after six o'clock.
WILLIAM TAYLOR ( police-constable M 79.) In consequence of a description given to me, I went to Market-street, where the prisoner lived, and she opened the door—we told her we came about the child—she said she had heard of it, but knew nothing about it, that she had been out every day from eight o'clock in the morning till night—this ring I got from Mary Jones at the Town-hall.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you find this handkerchief? A. At the pawnbroker's—I had known the prisoner before—I have had no quarrel with her—no more than when she has been in a disturbance—I did not say I would send her over the water if I could—Mrs. Cowdroy described the prisoner to me as a stoutish woman, with carrotty hair—that she had her hair plain, and she had a green gown on and a strawbonnet, and a child in her arms—she gave no other description—I am sure of that—she described no other article of dress—she did not say she had a dark shawl with a flower in the corner—not to my recollection.
JOHN RICHARD COWLIN ( police-sergeant M 5.) I went with Taylor to take the prisoner.
Prisoner Defence I am innocent.
DEBORAH BROWN . I am married. I knew the prisoner from a child—on Wednesday, the 6th of July last, I was going to Billigsgate, with another person—as we were coming back, I and Mrs. Neale went into
the Swan public-house, about five o'clock—I saw the prisoner when we went in, sitting down with her husband, eating a piece of duck or fowl in her hand—she had a green bonnet, a dark gown, and shawl she has on how—I am sure the bonnet was green—I staid there till seven o'clock—she was there all the time.
MR. DOANE. Q. What is your husband? A. A porter, and resides at No. 60, Broad-row, Prospect-place—I was not in the habit of visiting the prisoner—the Swan public-house is on Fish-street-hill, just by the foot of the bridge—I recollect this was Wednesday, because she was taken the next night—I did not hear it till Friday morning—she was taken on Thursday night—I have never stated this till now—I said I was quite surprised, how could that be, when I saw her there?—I said so at her mother's—I do not live far from her mother's—I was in the habit of going there—I am not related in any way to any part of the family—I am quite sure it was the Swan, to the best of my knowledge—I have known Mrs. Neale a long time—it was quite accidentally that I saw the prisoner there—I got there about five o'clock—Mrs. Neale and I sat together, and this woman and her husband and two young men, Richard Smith and John Flemming, were there—no one else was there that I know of—there might be other persons—I know these young men—I do not know whether they were friends of Mrs. Neale—she appeared to know them—we all conversed together about different things—the prisoner mentioned that her feet were blistered with walking, that she had been out with her husband—she did not say where she had been—we talked of other subjects which I should not like to mentioned—I said, "I must go home—it is getting late—I am afraid my husband will get home before me"—I had some porter to drink—we all drank together out of the same pot—nothing else besides porter—no gin or brandy—I and Mrs. Neale went first away—we left the two young men, and this woman and her husband there—I put down 4 d. for one pot, and paid no more—if Mrs. Neale paid any thing I did not see it.
BRIDGET NEALE . I know Deborah Brown. On Wednesday, the 6th of July, I went into the Swan public-house with Brown about five minutes to five o'clock, to the best of my knowledge—there were several persons in the house, men and woman—the prisoner was there with her husband—I think it was a piece of duck she had—she had a green merino bonnet, a dark cotton gown and a brown shawl—we staid there till seven o'clock—we had porter to drink—Mrs. Brown paid for a pot of porter—we drank it between us—the rest were drinking also, to the best of my knowledge—I have known the prisoner fourteen years—I cannot be mistaken in her person—we alkted together.
MR. DOANE. Q. How came you to go this place? A. I went to Billingsgate; and on returning we went into the Swan—I did not expect to meet any one there—I took notice of the time because I heard of the prisoner being detected—I said to Mrs. Brown. "I won't stop very long, because my husband will be here," but conversation came on, and we delayed—we were talking about family affairs—her husband was there—the prisoner and I sat together, and her husband—not exactly on the same form, but we kept together—Smith and Flemming were there—the prisoner complained about her corns or something, or her shoes pinching—I am not at all related to her—I did not hear of this till Saturday—my husband told me—I saw her husband after she was in custody, on Monday
I believe, and mentioned it to him—Mrs. Brown was not present when I saw the husband on Monday—I saw Mrs. Brown on the Wednesday following, and then we talked this matter over.
NOT GUILTY

2008. GEORGE TURNER and EDWARD-FLEETWOOD were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; the goods of Samuel Medley, from his person; and that the said Edward Fleetwood had been before convicted of felony.
SAMUEL MEDLEY . I was in High-street, Southwark, on the 21st of July, about eight o'clock in the evening—the office spoke to me, and I missed a handkerchief from my pocket—this is it—it is marked.
JAMES ANDREWS ( police-constable M 45.) I was on duty near St. George's church, and saw the two prisoners—I watched them, and saw them follow a gentleman as far as St. Thomas's street—Turner attempted a gentleman's pocket, but did not take any thing—they them crossed, and followed the prosecutor, who was walking with two other people—Turner took this handkerchief and gave it to Fleetwood—they crossed over to where I was—I took them, and Fleetwood threw down the handkerchief.
Tuner. I was thirty yards from this young man when he took him, and then a butcher took me—it was dark, he could not see me. Witness. it was half-past eight o'clock when I first saw them—it was nearly nine when this happened.
JAMES SOUNDY ( City police constable No. 53) I produce certificate of the conviction of Fleetwood, by the name of George Watkins, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—the prisoner is the person.
Fleetwood. This young man is quite innocent—the person that was with me is not here—he was a good deal like Turner.
James Andrews re-examined. Q. Have you the slightest doubt that Turner is the man that put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, and took the handkerchief? A. I am quite positive he is.
Turner's Defence. I was walking home—if I had known I had stolen
a handkerchief, I would have run away, but the officer passed by me and took Fleetwood—I never was charged with such a thing in my life—I work hard for my living—I had been down to Mr. Blackbird, a pastry-cook, and was returning home.
TURNER— GUILTY . Aged 20— Confined Nine Months.
FLEETWOOD— GUILTY . Aged 19— Transported For Fourteen Years.

2009. SAMUEL BLACKLEY and ROBERT ALLPIN were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, 1 pair of shoes, value 3 s., the goods of John Saul; and that Robert Allpin had been before convicted of felony: and ANN SMITH for feloniously receiving the same goods, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c., and that she had been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD LANGLEY ( police-sergeant Q. 11) On the 11th of August, I was going along Blackman-street, and saw the two male prisoners go to Mr. Saul's shop—Blackley took a pair of shoes, and handed them to Allpin—they separated—I pointed out Blackley to a policeman to take him—while I was doing that, Allpin turned a corner, and I lost him.
THEOPHILUS COOPER . I am servant to Mr. Saul, he is a sale-shop keeper, in Blackman-street, Borough. He sells shoes which hang out of the door—I missed a pair of shoes on that day—these are them.
Allpin Q. How far were you from me? A. About twelve of fifteen yards—I did not say fifty yards at Union Hall.
WILLIAM ALEXANDER JONES . I am a pawnbroker, and produce the shoes—Smith pawned them for 1 s —she said her name was Taylor.
Smith. He has received my things, and my children's many times,
PATRICK GLYNN ( police-constable R 170.) I took Blackley, and found the duplicate of these shoes in his pocket.
WILLIAM BOWMAN ( police-constable M 162.) I produce the certificate of Smith's former conviction, from the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey ( read)—she is the woman.
JOHN RIORDAN ( police-constable M 63.) I produce the certificate of Allpin's former conviction ( read)—he is the man.
Blackley's Defence. I was standing by the church—a young man came by and offered me them for 1 s. 6 d.—I said I would give him 9 d., which was all I had—he took it—I went to a place, and found this young woman, who took them to pawn for me.
Allpin's Defence. I never saw the things at all, till I came to Union Hall—the officer said he only saw my coat, and he was fifty yards off—he said he was afraid to make a noise.
EDWARD LANGLEY . I did not—I am sure, they are the two men—I watched them for about five minutes—I saw them talk and walk together.
BLACKLEY— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
ALLPIN— GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported For Fourteen Years.
SMITH— NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18360815-2010]
2010. THOMAS CARTHY, JOHN CARTHY , and THOMAS MARSH , were indicted for a misdemeanour.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH STOCKWELL . I am servant to Mr. Jones, of Broomfield-place.
Deptford. On Friday evening, the 15th of July, I was in the shop—the prisoner Marsh came in for a 1 d. worth of tobacco—I told him my master did not deal in that—and then he asked for a penny pie—I served him—he gave me a sixpence—I showed it to master—it turned out to be bad—I told him it was bad—he said he had taken it for his work—I gave it him back and he left.
ANN BRADY . I live at Deptford. On the 15th of July, I was assisting Mrs. Owen, a potato-dealer there—two young men came up to the shop shed—I do not know them myself (I showed them to Mr. Males the officer) they gave me sixpence—I gave it to Mr. Males, where I went to get it changed—I took it back to the young man, and they gave me a good one—they bought a 1 d. worth of potatoes—they went away and Males followed them.
WILLIAM MALES , Jun. I was at the shop door—Ann Brady pointed out Marsh, and John Carthy to me—Brady gave me a bad sixpence—she took the bad one back and they gave her a good one—she brought it to me—the two young men went away together.
WILLIAM MALES , Sen. I live at Deptford, and am the father of the last witness. I recollect Mrs. Brady coming to my son for change for sixpence—I followed the two young men—I saw them first go to widow Townsend's, the baker's—one of them went it—I followed them after he came out, to the Black Horse Bridge, they were then joined by the prisoner Thomas Carthy—they then went towards Rotherhithe—I sent to William Badham, and he joined us at China-hall—when he joined us the three prisoners separated, and one went inside Mr. Mumford's shop—after he came out they fell into company again—they went two hundred yards and then went to a bun-shop—one went in—he came out and they joined again—I went into the bun-shop, came out, and followed them—I saw Marsh and John Carthy go into a pork-shop—Thomas Carthy did not go into any shop, to my knowledge—I still followed them over Mill-pond Bridge—I sent to the station-house that I wanted more assistance—they joined again after they got through St. James's-gate—we and the policeman then surrounded them, put them into a baker's-shop near Lilliput-hall, and took them to the station-house—I searched Thomas Carthy, and found on him four base sixpences, and 2 s. 10 1/2 d. in copper.
WILLIAM BADHAM . I am a police-constable. I joined Males in pursuit of the prisoners—when they were taken, I saw Thomas Carthy stoop down, and do something to his left foot—when we got to the station-house, I examined him, and found three pieces of metal on him—three counterfeit sixpences fell from his shoe—I found a file and knife on John Carthy.
Thomas Marsh. There was no money tumbled from my shoe, but it fell from my jacket pocket.
JOSIAH PERCEVALL . I assisted in taking the prisoners—I searched Marsh, and found, 1 s. 6 d. in good silver, and 6 1/2 d. in copper, and a duplicate.
WILLIAM PHIPPS . I am an inspector of police. I saw the shoe taken of Thomas Carthy—I heard something fall, and picked it up—it was three counterfeit sixpences.
JOHN FIELD . I am an inspector of coin to the Mint. These sixpences are all six counterfeit—four of them are unfinished—a file and knife would be useful to put them in a state for circulation—this white metal is the same as the money is made of—it is what we call the get —the other three sixpences
are also counterfeit, but the metal has been removed from the edges of them.
Charthy's Defence. We went to sell mats, and sold them to a wagoner, who gave us 4 s. in half pence—we met this young man—he asked it we were going to London—we said yes, and he picked up this money and gave it me—I did not know whether it was good or bad—I put it into my waistcoat pocket with some copper.
Thomas Marsh's Defence. I picked up a rag, and in that was a paper, and in that seven bad sixpences—I gave them to Thomas Carthy, and we had not got three yards further before we picked up three pieces of metal, which Thomas Carthy took.
THOMAS CARTHY— GUILTY . Aged 19.
JOHN CARTHY— GUILTY . Aged 17.
THOMAS MARSH— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Confined Eighteen Months.
Fifth Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360815-2011]
2011. GEORGE TEBBUTT was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
LEWIS GRAVE . I am in partnership with Mr. William Winterbourne—we are linen and woollen-drapers, in Blackman-street, Borough. The prisoner came into our service about March—we do the tally trade to a considerable extent, and are obliged to employ persons to make collections by the week—I have got the book that the prisoner kept in his travels—it was his duty to receive money on our account, and to enter it in this book, and account to me every night—his particular days for collecting were Monday and Tuesday, and it was his duty to enter in this book, the sums received on each of the days mentioned, in this column from the person whose surname appears—there is no entry of 2 s. from Mrs. Stubbs on the 5th of April; nor of 2 s. from Mrs. Carpenter on the 16th of May, or the 27th of June—Stubbs' was a Tuesday's collection, and Carpenter's a Monday's—we have some of Mrs. Carpenter's bills which are in the book—it was the duty of the prisoner to enter on the back of each bill of parcels, the date and sum received by the week—on Mrs. Carpenter's bill there is a sum of 2 s. on the 16th of May, and 27th of June, but they are not in the book, and he never accounted to me for them—the writing on the bills is his—on the 29th of June he did not come, and a letter was received by Mr. Winterbourne.
Prisoner. You say I received Stubbs' on Tuesday, it was on Wednesday, because the round was too long.
ELIZA STUBBS . I am single, and live in Lambeth-place, West-square. I was in the habit of paying sums of money to the prisoner on account of his employers—he came sometimes on Tuesday, sometimes on Wednesday—this is my bill—on the 5th of April, I paid him 2 s., and here is his writing on the back of the bill at the time I paid it.
Prisoner. Q. When you paid me on the Wednesday morning, I dated it as if received on the Tuesday? A. Yes, you did.
SARAH CARPENTER . I am the wife of Edward Carpenter, of Adams-place, Rotherhithe. I had been in the habit of paying the prisoner, on account of his master, sums of money weekly for articles—on the 16th of May, Monday, I paid him 2 s., he entered it on the back of the bill—on the 27th of June I paid him 2 s., he entered that also on the back of the bill—I remember his coming to me on Sunday—I think it was in the
month of July—he told me his governor was coming on the Monday, and I was not to pay him, nor yet to give him any bill—I asked him what was the reason—he said he had had a bit of a spree, and he had spent his master's money; but in the course of a week or two he should be able to recover all that—I made him no answer—when my husband came home, I told him—his master called the next day—the prisoner came again on the Monday following, and gave me a new bill—the old bill contained as usual the indorsement on the back of it, showing the weekly sums received up to the 13th of June—in the old bill there appears to have been received 13 s. 6 d., and in the new bill there is cash received 11 s. 6 d., but I had paid him 13 s. 6 d.—he told me to destroy the old bill, there was no occasion to keep it—he did not ask me to give it him—he told me to burn it, it was no good—I showed it to my husband, and in consequence of what he told me, I kept both—he used to have such a red covered book as that in which he made his entries.
Prisoner. I told you there was an error in the bill of 2 s.—I did not know where it was—I put down 2 s., but you did not pay me that 2 s. Witness. Yes, I did.
HENRY DOUGHTON WINTERBOURNE . I am in partnership with Mr. Grave. On Tuesday the 28th of June, it was the prisoner's duty to attend in the evening as usual with his collecting book—instead of coming, he sent a letter about half-past ten o'clock in the evening—I took no charge of the letter, and cannot find it—it was not in his handwriting—in consequence of what the letter contained, I went to his residence, No. 3, Crown-square, Borough—he was not there—according to the note, I expected to find him ill in bed—I found him in the street dancing with a pipe in his mouth—I did not let him know I saw him—he has not accounted to me for my of the sums that have been mentioned.
Prisoner. Q. Have not these sums been accounted for an paid in your books? A. They have not.
Prisoner's Defence. ( written.) "My Lord, and gentleman of the Jury. Those deficiencies frequently occur in that tally trade, as I have upwards of 150 persons to call on every day; at the same time those amounts brought against me have been accounted for to my employers, excepting the two last sums of 4 s. and 2 s., which would have been placed to my account, as others were, at the end of the week; and they also agreed to charge me with those amounts, my having a small account open for goods had by my wife, and to be deducted at 3 s. per week from my salary, which was regularly done up to the 25th of June, 1836."
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Seven Years.
THERE WERE TWO OTHER INDICTMENTS AGAINST THE PRISONER.
[trialAccount t18360815-2012]
2012. THOMAS SUTCH was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of June, 1 hammer, value 1 s. 4 d.; 2 pump-irons, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 seatiron, value 1 s.; 1 fore-part iron, value 1 s.; 1 strap, value 8 d.; 1 knife, value 3 d.; 4 awls, value 4 d.1 long-stick, value 1 d.; 2 files, value 10 d., 1 brush, value 2 d.; 1 stirrup, value 3 d.; 1 lap-iron, value 2 d.; and the materials for a pair of uppers for shoes, value 1 s. 3 d.; the goods of Thomas Lansbrough Drew, his master, and that he had ben before convicted of felony.
THOMAS LANSBROUGH DREW . I live in Assembly-street, Blackfriar's-road, and am a shoemaker. The prisoner was my apprentice—I came
down on the morning of the 28th of June, at half-past five o'clock and he and the tools stated were gone—the prisoner was apprehended and brought to me by the officer, with these tools, on the 16th of July.
Prisoner. You know you ill-used me—I can show the marks—the tools were part of them mine. Witness The part that were his were taken away at Queen-square—the rest that are here are mine.
EDWARD JAMES LEVY ( police-sergeant T 18.) I took the prisoner at Brentford—these tools were found in the room where he was at work—he said they were his.
THOMAS HOBBS ( police-constable C 85.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—the prisoner is the man—( read)
(John Sutch, the prisoner's uncle, gave him a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported For Fourteen Years.
Sixth Jury, Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18360815-2013]
2013. CATHERINE NEALE was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of July, 1 shift, value 1 s., the goods of Norah Shea: 2 caps, value 6 s., the goods of Mary Mack: 1 bonnet, value 6 s.; 1 gown, value 7 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 4 s.; 1 waist-ribbon, value 6 d.; the goods of Ellen Carty: 8 yards of printed cotton, value 8 s.; 1 apron, value 10 d.; the goods of Mary Connell: 1 gown, value 6 d.; 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 2 s.; 1 pair of gloves, value 6 d.; and 1 cap, value 6 d.; the goods of Ellen Rowan.
NORAH SHEA . I lost my shift from Mr. Biggs, a market-gardener, at Barnet—I had left it on line, over my bed—I slept in a barn there (I came over from Ireland two years ago) the prisoner worked there about three months—I found the shift on her person—I never lent it to her.
MARY MACK . I work for Mr. Biggs, and sleep in the same place—I missed two caps—the policeman has them—these are mine.
ELLEN CARTY . I worked for Mr. Biggs three or four years—I slept in the same barn—I lost a bonnet, a gown, three handkerchiefs, and a waistribbon—these are mine.
MARY CONNELL . I worked for Mr. Biggs, and slept in the barn—I lost a gown-piece and apron—these are them—I never lent them to the prisoner—I only had this gown-piece from about one o'clock on Tuesday till the Wednesday night, when I lost it.
ELLEN ROWAN . I worked in the same place. I missed a gown, a petticoat, cap, and gloves—these are them—I kept them in the barn over my bed on a line—I never lent them to the prisoner.
MARGARET KIRWAN . I live on Ealing-common, near the Uxbridge-road, and keep a house to accommodate travellers. I have no sign up—the prisoner came there on Wednesday night, and brought this property—the policeman took her on the Sunday morning.
JOHN PASCOE ( police-sergeant I 19.) I found some of this property of Kirwan's some on the prisoner's person, and I found a duplicate of some of it on her.
JOHN ALDOUS . I am a pawnbroker at Hammersmith. This gown and gown-piece were pawned at my house—I don't know by whom, but this duplicate was given for it.
Prisoner. I asked to borrow a gown and handkerchief—I was going
to see a friend—we were always in the habit of lending to each other any thing we had.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360815-2014]
2014. THOMAS WHEELER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of August, 1 purse, value 2 d.; 1 sixpence, and 7 pence, and 7 half-pence, the goods and monies of Charles Simpson, from the person of Rebecca Simpson; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
REBECCA SIMPSON . I am the wife of Charles Simpson, of Brothers-row, Lambeth. On the 1st of August I was at Lambeth-stairs, looking at the rowing match, and had a red purse in my pocket containing the money stated—a gentleman came and told me something—I was my purse on the ground—a gentleman seized the prisoner—this is my purse—it has the money in it now.
GEORGE VICKERS . ( police-constable L 54.) About seven o'clock in the evening I was on duty, and saw the prisoner standing close behind the prosecutrix—I did not see him take the purse out of her pocket, but I took his hand from her person, and took this purse out of his hand—he let it fall on the ground.
WILLIAM ROWE ( police-sergeant M 10.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported For Fourteen Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney
MIDDLESEX CASE.
[trialAccount t18360815-2015]
2015. WILLIAM MATTHEWS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 12 umbrellas, value 2 l., the goods of Thomas Evans.
ELIZA BRUCE . I am in the employ of Thomas Evans. On the 12th of August, I was taking twelve umbrellas to him, Silver-street, at eight o'clock in the evening—I put them in the passage, where we always put the work, by the warehouse-door, and sat down about half a yard from them—my attention was called by a young woman, and they were gone—I went after a man in white trowsers, who had come into the passage—I spoke to him, and said, "You have got my work"—he said something—I saw the prisoner in Addle-street, and saw him drop my umbrellas—he was running at the time—I hallooed out, "Stop thief"—he was stopped, but not in my presence—the umbrellas were picked up, and given to me—I was about four yards from the prisoner, on the other side of the road, at the time—I saw him again in about ten minutes.
THOMAS FREESTON KIRBY . I live in Addle-street. On the evening of the 12th of August I saw the prisoner in Addle-street, with a bundle of umbrellas—he was running—I saw him drop them—I heard the prosecutrix cry, "Stop thief," and ran after him till he was taken, by a person, at the end of Philip-lane, and given to an officer—he had run about two hundred yards after dropping them, and turned a corner—I was so close to him, and did not lose sight of him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you keep sight of him all the time? A. Yes—the person who took him is not here—he was given into the custody of an officer—I was about thirty yards from him when he
was stopped—the moment he dropped the umbrellas, he ran away so fast it was almost impossible any body could run so swift—the corner is not twenty yards from where he dropped the umbrellas—he had dark trowsers on—I had never seen him before.
FRANCIS EARL . I am a constable of Cripplegate. I heard the cry of "Stop thief" and saw the prisoner running down Phillip-lane—I endeavoured to stop him, but missed my hold—I turned round and pursued him—another person, a stranger, caught hold of him—I came up almost immediately and secured him—Kirby came to the station-house directly afterwards—I did not notice him before.
Cross-examined. Q. He did not come up where the prisoner was stopped? A. I did notice—him—there were a great many people there.
THOMAS EVAN . I am the employer of Bruce—the umbrellas are mine. Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A wholesale umbrella-manufacturer—these are not finished, but they are complete enough to answer the purpose of umbrellas.
Prisoner's Defence. I was returning from my master's—I went to my mother's and on coming back, I was going to Billingsgate to assist my wife home, and on the road in Wood-street I heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I ran with the other people—a man in white trowsers was running, and that man let the bundle fall out of his arm—I turned one way, and suppose the man went the other—as soon as I turned the corner I was laid hold of—four or five people came to the station-house, and said I was not the man who dropped it, that it was a person in white trowsers; but they were all pushed away from the door—the door was shut in their faces—they promised to come forward, but I do not know whether they are here.
MARY ANN FORD . I live in Phillip-lane, my husband works in Aldersgate-street. I was in Silver-street on the 12th of August, and as I came across from Silver-street, there was a young man with a bundle of umbrellas under his left arm—they were covered with green baize—the young man came across the road, and his right arm touched my left—an umbrella-maker living in our court, made me take notice, and I followed him down opposite the locksmith's—the people came running down the street—he turned his head, saw them running, and threw down the umbrellas in the gutter, a few yards from me—I did not go to the watch-house when the prisoner was taken—I do not know him—the man had dirty looking white trowsers on—the prisoner is not the young man I saw throw down the umbrellas—I cannot say it was not him—I saw no features because I was behind him, but I am sure he had dirty looking white trowsers—when they were thrown down there was a cry of "Stop thief"—there were several persons standing who might have taken him, but I could not see his face.
JAMES BOLTON . I am an appraiser, and live in Addle-street. On the 12th of August, I was returning home from Aldermanbury—I had arrived near the end of Phillip-lane, when I heard the cry of "Stop thief", and immediately afterwards saw two men run past me, one in white trowsers, and the other in dark—the one in white trowsers was on the right hand side, and the other on the left—I turned after them, and went down to the end of the street—they then parted, the man in white trowsers to the right, and the other to the left—two girls were following them—I returned towards my own home, and when I got to Philip-lane, I found somebody in custody—I then went to the station-house, and was refused admission—I really cannot say whether the prisoner was either of those men.
THOMAS KIRBY re-examined There were two men running—one in white trowsers, and the prisoner in dark trowsers—I stopped the man in white trowers because he was nearest to me—he said, "It is not me," and I saw the prisoner with the bundle under his arm—I let the man in white trowsers go—and followed the prisoner and saw him drop the bundle.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why not tell his Lordship in the first instance that you stopped the man in white trowsers? A. The question was not put to me.
JOHN BOLTON . No one was passing, no one pursued or stopped the man in white trowsers I solemnly declare—nobody could stop from the end of Phillip-lane to Aldermanbury.
THOMAS KIRBY re-examined. It was in Addle-street I stopped him—the man who dropped the umbrellas was not twenty yards from me at the time—I did not lose sight of him till he was taken—the other took the right hand side, and this man the left, and I took him—he is the man who was taken to the station-house.
ELIZA BRUCE re-examined. I saw the prisoner drop the umbrella—I did not see the face, but he is the person—I spoke to the man with white trowsers and said, "You have got my work," he said, "No; it is not me, I have not got your work"—so I turned my head, and saw this man running with them—the man in white trowsers came into the passage, and I thought it was him at first, and followed, and spoke to him.
THOMAS KIRBY . He dropped the umbrellas before he turned the corner—that was the time he gained ground on me—I positively did not lose sight of him—I saw the prisoner at the station-house.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Four Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 19 TH OF SEPTEMBER
